Translation by periods of
{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)

[
63
, p.123]

\displaystyle\theta_{m,n}(z+2\rho)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{jm+\ell n}(q^{-1}e^{-2i\pi z})^{\ell}\theta_{m,n}(z)

(20)

\displaystyle\theta_{m,n}(z-2\rho)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{jm+\ell n}(q^{-1}e^{2i\pi z})^{\ell}\theta_{m,n}(z).

(21)

4.

Translation by half-periods of
{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)

[
63
, Table 0, p.19]

\displaystyle\theta_{m,n}(z+\rho)

\displaystyle=(q^{-1/4}e^{-i\pi z})^{\ell}i^{\ell(n+j)}(-1)^{\ell(n+j)+j\ell n+jmn}\theta_{m+\ell,n+j}(z)

(22)

\displaystyle\theta_{m,n}(z-\rho)

\displaystyle=(q^{-1/4}e^{i\pi z})^{\ell}i^{\ell(n+j)}(-1)^{jm+j\ell n+jmn}\theta_{m+\ell,n+j}(z).

(23)

We need the following addition formula. A proof is provided in Lemma
72
of Appendix
A
since we could not find it as such in the literature.

Lemma 18
.

For
m,n\in\{0,1\}
with indices understood mod 2, and
z,w\in{\mathbb{C}}
,

\begin{split}\theta_{1,1}^{2}(z)\theta_{0,0}^{2}(w)+&(-1)^{1+m+mn}\theta_{m,n}^{2}(z)\theta_{m+1,n+1}^{2}(w)=\\
&=(-1)^{n+mn}\theta_{m+1,n+1}(z+w)\theta_{m+1,n+1}(z-w)\theta_{m,n}^{2}(0).\end{split}

(24)

We also need the following result. A proof is provided in Lemma
73
of Appendix
A
since we use a slight extension which is not written as such in
[
63
]
.

Lemma 19
.

[
63
, §6, p.24]

Let
a_{1},\dots,a_{r},b_{1},\dots,b_{r}\in{\mathbb{C}}
. Then, the function

f(u)=e^{-2i\pi\ell u}\prod_{i=1}^{r}\frac{\theta_{m,n}(u-a_{i})}{\theta_{m,n}(u-b_{i})},

is a meromorphic function on
{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)
if and only if
\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, and
\sum_{i=1}^{r}(a_{i}-b_{i})-\ell\tau\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Modular transformations of the lattice parameters.

We recall the required notions, and refer to
[
56
, Ch.9]
for more details.
Modular transformations
consist of the following transformations of the modular parameter
\tau
:

\tau^{\prime}=\frac{c+d\tau}{a+b\tau},

where
a,b,c,d\in{\mathbb{Z}}
satisfy
ad-bc=1
. They can be seen as the action of the group
SL_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
on the upper half plane by Möbius transformations. They thus form a group, known as the
modular group
, and we have
{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)={\mathbb{T}}(\tau^{\prime})
. The
standard fundamental domain
is:

\{\tau\in{\mathbb{C}},\Im(\tau)>0\,:\,\Re(\tau)\leq\frac{1}{2},|\tau|\geq 1\}.

(25)

Generators of the modular group are given by:

\tau_{1}=\tau_{1}(\tau)=-\frac{1}{\tau}\quad;\quad\tau_{2}=\tau_{2}(\tau)=\tau+1.

The following lemma records the evolution of theta functions under modular transformations.

Lemma 20
.

1.

Under the transformation
\tau_{1}=-\frac{1}{\tau}

[
25
, (20.7.30)-(20.7.32)]
,

\displaystyle(-i\tau)^{\frac{1}{2}}\theta_{1,1}(z,\tau)=-ie^{i\pi\tau_{1}z^{2}}\theta_{1,1}(\tau_{1}\cdot z,\tau_{1})\penalty

\displaystyle,\penalty\ (-i\tau)^{\frac{1}{2}}\theta_{0,0}(z,\tau)=e^{i\pi\tau_{1}z^{2}}\theta_{0,0}(\tau_{1}\cdot z,\tau_{1})

(26)

\displaystyle(-i\tau)^{\frac{1}{2}}\theta_{1,0}(z,\tau)=-ie^{i\pi\tau_{1}z^{2}}\theta_{0,1}(\tau_{1}\cdot z,\tau_{1})\penalty

\displaystyle,\penalty\ (-i\tau)^{\frac{1}{2}}\theta_{0,1}(z,\tau)=e^{i\pi\tau_{1}z^{2}}\theta_{1,0}(\tau_{1}\cdot z,\tau_{1}).

2.

Under the transformation
\tau_{2}=\tau+1

[
25
, (20.7.26)-(20.7.28)]
,

\theta_{1,1}(z,\tau)=e^{-i\frac{\pi}{4}}\theta_{1,1}(z,\tau_{2}),\quad\theta_{0,0}(z,\tau)=\theta_{0,1}(z,\tau_{2}).

(27)

3.1.2
Definition and first properties

We are now ready to state the definition of Fock’s dimer model
[
32
]
, and establish some properties.

Definition 21

(
[
32
]
)
.

Consider an infinite minimal graph
\mathsf{G}
, a modular parameter
\tau
, an angle map
\bm{\alpha}:\vec{\mathscr{T}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
, and an element
t
of
{\mathbb{C}}
. Then,
Fock’s dimer model on
\mathsf{G}
in genus 1, with parameters
\tau,\bm{\alpha},t
, or simply
Fock’s dimer model
, is defined through its Kasteleyn matrix
\mathsf{K}_{\bm{\alpha},t}(\,\cdot\,,\tau)=\mathsf{K}_{\bm{\alpha},t}(\,\cdot\,)
, referred to as
Fock’s Kasteleyn matrix
. The latter has rows indexed by white vertices of
\mathsf{G}
, columns by black ones, and non-zero coefficients given by, for
every edge
\mathsf{w}\mathsf{b}
of
\mathsf{G}
,

\mathsf{K}_{\bm{\alpha},t}((\mathsf{w},\mathsf{b}),\tau)=\mathsf{K}_{\bm{\alpha},t}(\mathsf{w},\mathsf{b})=\frac{\theta_{1,1}(\beta-\alpha)}{\theta_{0,0}(t+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f}))\theta_{0,0}(t+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f}^{\prime}))},

(28)

where the edge
\mathsf{w}\mathsf{b}
is crossed by train-tracks with angles
\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{C}}
, see Figure
3
(left); suppose that intersecting train-tracks have distinct angles for otherwise the coefficient is 0.
Depending on the context, we will emphasize the dependence in
\tau
in the notation for
\mathsf{K}
, or not.

Suppose that Kasteleyn-Kuperberg phases
\phi
are assigned to edges of
\mathsf{G}
. Then, by Definition (
2
), Fock’s Kasteleyn matrix
\mathsf{K}_{\bm{\alpha},t}
defines a dimer weight function, which is
complex-valued
in general.

\begin{overpic}[fig_minimal.pdf]
\put(-2.0,11.0){\scriptsize$\mathsf{w}$}
\put(19.5,11.0){\scriptsize$\mathsf{b}$}
\put(2.0,18.5){\scriptsize$\beta$}
\put(15.0,18.5){\scriptsize$\alpha$}
\put(9.0,4.0){\scriptsize$\mathsf{f}$}
\put(9.0,18.0){\scriptsize$\mathsf{f}^{\prime}$}
\put(48.0,26.2){\scriptsize$\alpha_{1}$}
\put(41.0,26.0){\scriptsize$\beta_{1}$}
\put(30.5,8.0){\scriptsize$\alpha_{2}$}
\put(36.0,-1.0){\scriptsize$\beta_{2}$}
\put(54.0,0.0){\scriptsize$\alpha_{3}$}
\put(61.0,8.0){\scriptsize$\beta_{3}$}
\put(39.0,23.0){\scriptsize$\mathsf{w}_{1}$}
\put(32.0,11.0){\scriptsize$\mathsf{b}_{1}$}
\put(39.0,0.5){\scriptsize$\mathsf{w}_{2}$}
\put(51.0,0.5){\scriptsize$\mathsf{b}_{2}$}
\put(58.0,11.0){\scriptsize$\mathsf{w}_{3}$}
\put(51.0,23.0){\scriptsize$\mathsf{b}_{3}$}
\put(45.5,9.0){\scriptsize$\mathsf{f}$}
\put(84.0,9.0){\scriptsize{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})$}}
\put(62.5,20.0){\scriptsize{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{2}-\alpha_{1}$}}
\put(80.0,27.0){\scriptsize{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{1}-\alpha_{1}$}}
\put(94.0,20.0){\scriptsize{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{1}-\alpha_{3}$}}
\put(94.0,3.0){\scriptsize{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{3}-\alpha_{3}$}}
\put(80.0,-2.0){\scriptsize{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{3}-\alpha_{2}$}}
\put(64.0,3.0){\scriptsize{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{2}-\alpha_{2}$}}
\end{overpic}

Figure 3
:
Left: train-track angles
\alpha,\beta
at an edge
\mathsf{w}\mathsf{b}
. Center: train-track angles
\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\dots,\alpha_{|\mathsf{f}|/2},\beta_{|\mathsf{f}|/2}
around a face
\mathsf{f}
of degree
6
, with vertices
\mathsf{w}_{1},\mathsf{b}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{w}_{|\mathsf{f}|/2},\mathsf{b}_{|\mathsf{f}|/2}
in counterclockwise order. Right (blue): computation of the discrete Abel map using
\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})
.

As noted in Section
2.1.1
, rather than the weight function, the relevant quantities defining the dimer model are the face-weights.
Given a face
\mathsf{f}
of degree
|\mathsf{f}|
(where
|\mathsf{f}|
is even since
\mathsf{G}
is bipartite) with vertices labeled
\mathsf{w}_{1},\mathsf{b}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{w}_{|\mathsf{f}|/2},\mathsf{b}_{|\mathsf{f}|/2}
in counterclockwise order, and train-track angles denoted by
\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{|\mathsf{f}|/2},\beta_{|\mathsf{f}|/2},
as in Figure
3
(center), the face-weight
\mathscr{W}_{\bm{\alpha},t}(\mathsf{f},\tau)=\mathscr{W}_{\bm{\alpha},t}(\mathsf{f})
associated to Fock’s dimer model is given by

\mathscr{W}_{\bm{\alpha},t}(\mathsf{f},\tau)=\mathscr{W}_{\bm{\alpha},t}(\mathsf{f})=\prod_{i=1}^{|\mathsf{f}|/2}\frac{\theta_{1,1}(\alpha_{i}-\beta_{i+1})}{\theta_{1,1}(\beta_{i}-\alpha_{i})}\frac{\theta_{0,0}(t+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{i}-\alpha_{i})}{\theta_{0,0}(t+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{i+1}-\alpha_{i})},

(29)

where we used the definition of the discrete Abel map to express all terms involving
\mathbf{d}
using
\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})
, see Figure
3
(right).

Theta functions have non-trivial periods along horizontal/vertical periods of the torus
{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)
so that, in order for Fock’s Kasteleyn matrix to be well defined, the angle map
\bm{\alpha}
and the parameter
t
have to be considered as living in
{\mathbb{C}}
. The next lemma proves that actually face-weights are periodic functions on
{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)
of the parameters of the model. This implies that
when considering face-weights, the angle map
\bm{\alpha}
and
t
can be seen as living on
{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)
, whereas when considering Fock’s Kasteleyn matrix, lifted angles in
{\mathbb{C}}
should be considered.
We do not introduce different notation for the angles in
{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)
and the lifted angles in
{\mathbb{C}}
, trying to make it clear from the context. Note that periodicity of face-weights under horizontal translations is already proved in
[
13
, Remark 30]
.

Lemma 22
.

For every face
\mathsf{f}
of
\mathsf{G}
, the face-weight
\mathscr{W}_{\bm{\alpha},t}(\mathsf{f})
is a meromorphic function on the torus
{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)
when considered as a function of
t
, or of any of the angles
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}
,
i\in\{1,\dots,|\mathsf{f}|/2\}
.

Proof.

Consider first the face-weight
\mathscr{W}_{\bm{\alpha},t}(\mathsf{f})
as a function of
t
. Since the first term of
\mathscr{W}_{\bm{\alpha},t}(\mathsf{f})
is constant when seen as a function of
t
, we need to prove that

\prod_{i=1}^{|\mathsf{f}|/2}\frac{\theta_{0,0}(t+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{i}-\alpha_{i})}{\theta_{0,0}(t+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{i+1}-\alpha_{i})},

is a meromorphic function of
t
on
{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)
. This is indeed the case using Lemma
19
and observing that

\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathsf{f}|/2}\bigl(-\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})-\beta_{i}+\alpha_{i}\bigr)-\bigl(-\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})-\beta_{i+1}+\alpha_{i}\bigr)=\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathsf{f}|/2}(\beta_{i+1}-\beta_{i})=0.

Consider now the face-weight
\mathscr{W}_{\bm{\alpha},t}(\mathsf{f})
as a function of
\alpha_{i}
. Since the graph is minimal, we know that train-tracks around a face are distinct
[
11
, Lemma 8]
, so that
\alpha_{i}
corresponds to a single train-track around the face
\mathsf{f}
. We thus need to show that

\frac{\theta_{1,1}(\alpha_{i}-\beta_{i+1})}{\theta_{1,1}(\beta_{i}-\alpha_{i})}\frac{\theta_{0,0}(t+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{i}-\alpha_{i})}{\theta_{0,0}(t+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{i+1}-\alpha_{i})},

is a meromorphic function of
\alpha_{i}
on
{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)
. Using Identity (
22
), we have
that

\displaystyle\frac{\theta_{0,0}(t+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{i}-\alpha_{i})}{\theta_{0,0}(t+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{i+1}-\alpha_{i})}=e^{-i\pi(\beta_{i}-\beta_{i+1})}\frac{\theta_{1,1}(t-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\tau}{2}+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{i}-\alpha_{i})}{\theta_{1,1}(t-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\tau}{2}+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{i+1}-\alpha_{i})}.

Using furthermore that
\theta_{1,1}
is odd, we need to prove that

-e^{-i\pi(\beta_{i+1}-\beta_{i})}\frac{\theta_{1,1}(\alpha_{i}-\beta_{i+1})}{\theta_{1,1}(\alpha_{i}-\beta_{i})}\frac{\theta_{1,1}(\alpha_{i}-(t-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\tau}{2}+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{i}))}{\theta_{1,1}(\alpha_{i}-(t-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\tau}{2}+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{i+1}))},

is a meromorphic function of
\alpha_{i}
on
{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)
. The exponential term is constant when seen as a function of
\alpha_{i}
. For the remaining product of two terms, suppose first that there is no contribution
\alpha_{i}
to
\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})
, then this product is indeed meromorphic using Lemma
19
and observing that

\beta_{i+1}+\Bigl(t-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\tau}{2}+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{i}\Bigr)-\beta_{i}-\Bigl(t-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\tau}{2}+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{i+1}\Bigr)=0.

When
\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})
contains the term
\alpha_{i}
, the argument is similar, but involves heavier notation so that we do not make it explicit.
The proof is similar when considering the face-weight
\mathscr{W}_{\bm{\alpha},t}(\mathsf{f})
as a function of
\beta_{i}
. ∎

Remark 23
.

Note that the discrete Abel map
\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})
is always summed with
t
, so that our proof also implies that the face-weight
\mathscr{W}_{\bm{\alpha},t}(\mathsf{f})
is meromorphic when considered as function of
\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})
.

Lemma
24
below proves that Fock’s dimer model is invariant under modular transformations of the modular parameter
\tau
, up to an appropriate transformation of the angle map
\bm{\alpha}
and of the parameter
t
. In particular, this implies that we can restrict the study of Fock’s dimer model on the torus
{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)
to the standard fundamental domain of the modular parameter
\tau
as defined in Equation (
25
).

Lemma 24
.

For every face
\mathsf{f}
of
\mathsf{G}
, the face-weight
\mathscr{W}_{\bm{\alpha},t}(\mathsf{f},\tau)
transforms as follows under the two generators
\tau_{1}=-\frac{1}{\tau},\tau_{2}=\tau+1
of the modular group:

\mathscr{W}_{\bm{\alpha},t}(\mathsf{f},\tau)=\mathscr{W}_{\tau_{1}\bm{\alpha},\tau_{1}t}(\mathsf{f},\tau_{1}),\quad\mathscr{W}_{\bm{\alpha},t}(\mathsf{f},\tau)=\mathscr{W}_{\bm{\alpha},t+\frac{1}{2}}(\mathsf{f},\tau_{2}).

Proof.

The first equality is obtained by applying Identity (
26
) to
\mathscr{W}_{\bm{\alpha},t}(\mathsf{f},\tau)
. Observe that the theta characteristics
(1,1)
and
(0,0)
are unchanged, and that arguments of the theta functions are multiplied by
\tau_{1}
. The terms
(-i\tau)^{\frac{1}{2}}
and
(-i)
cancel out, so that we are left with proving that the contribution of the terms
e^{i\pi\tau_{1}z^{2}}
cancel out. The latter is equal to:

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{|\mathsf{f}|/2}e^{i\pi[(\alpha_{i}-\beta_{i+1})^{2}+(t+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{i}-\alpha_{i})^{2}-(\beta_{i}-\alpha_{i})^{2}-(t+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{i+1}-\alpha_{i})^{2}]}=e^{2i\pi\tau_{1}(t+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f}))\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathsf{f}|/2}(\beta_{i}-\beta_{i+1})}=1,

thus proving the first equality.
The second equality is obtained by noting that, when performing the modular transformation
\tau_{2}
, see Identity (
27
), arguments of theta functions are unchanged, the characteristic
(1,1)
is unchanged, and the characteristic
(0,0)
gets mapped to
(0,1)
. One then uses Identity (
22
) to bring
\theta_{0,1}
back to
\theta_{0,0}
:

\theta_{0,1}(z,\tau_{2})=\theta_{0,0}\Bigl(z+\frac{1}{2},\tau_{2}\Bigr).

The contributions of prefactors are all constant and cancel out, thus ending the proof of the second equality. ∎

3.2
Parameterization of real algebraic curves using dimers

Let
\mathscr{C}
be an algebraic curve of genus 1, and suppose that it satisfies Assumptions
(\dagger)
. By Fock
[
32
]
, there exists an infinite, periodic minimal graph
\mathsf{G}
, a torus
{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)
, a periodic angle map
\bm{\alpha}:\vec{\mathscr{T}_{1}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
, such that for every
t\in{\mathbb{C}}
, the spectral curve of Fock’s dimer model on
\mathsf{G}
is the curve
\mathscr{C}
up to a global scaling
(z,w)\leftrightarrow(\lambda z,\mu w)
for some
(\lambda,\mu)\in({\mathbb{C}}^{*})^{2}
. We speak of
Fock’s dimer models corresponding to the curve
\mathscr{C}
.
Note that, since the curve is supposed to be non singular apart from isolated real singularities, the angle map
\bm{\alpha}
is generic in the sense that there are a priori no relations between angles of distinct train-tracks, as linear relations for example.

Since the curve
\mathscr{C}
is irreducible, we actually know that an explicit bi-rational parameterization of
\mathscr{C}
is given by the meromorphic function
\Psi:{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
, where
\Psi(u)=(z(u),w(u))
, with
z,w
given by

z(u)=c_{1}\cdot e^{2i\pi\ell_{v}\cdot u}\prod_{\vec{T}\in\vec{\mathscr{T}}_{1}}\theta_{1,1}(u-\alpha_{\vec{T}})^{-v_{\vec{T}}},\quad w(u)=c_{2}\cdot e^{-2i\pi\ell_{h}\cdot u}\prod_{\vec{T}\in\vec{\mathscr{T}}_{1}}\theta_{1,1}(u-\alpha_{\vec{T}})^{h_{\vec{T}}},

(30)

for some
c_{1},c_{2}\in{\mathbb{C}}
, where
\sum_{\vec{T}\in\vec{\mathscr{T}}_{1}}v_{\vec{T}}=\sum_{\vec{T}\in\vec{\mathscr{T}}_{1}}h_{\vec{T}}=0
, and

\sum_{\vec{T}\in\vec{\mathscr{T}}_{1}}\alpha_{\vec{T}}v_{\vec{T}}=\ell_{v}\tau,\quad\sum_{\vec{T}\in\vec{\mathscr{T}}_{1}}\alpha_{\vec{T}}h_{\vec{T}}=\ell_{h}\tau,

(31)

with
\ell_{v},\ell_{h}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
; the form of the exponential prefactor in Equation (
30
) is given by Lemma
19
. The existence of
\Psi
follows from general parameterization theorems, while the breakthrough contribution of Fock
[
32
]
consists in relating parameters of
\Psi

to parameters of the dimer model on a periodic minimal graph
\mathsf{G}
with Fock’s Kasteleyn matrix, see Definition
21
. In the case of genus 0 Harnack curves, this had been previously established in

[
52
,
51
]
, and a specific case of the genus 1 case was established in
[
14
]
. Note that the construction of the periodic minimal graph
\mathsf{G}
arises from the paper
[
41
]
.

The algebraic curve
\mathscr{C}
is said to be
real
if it is invariant by complex conjugation:
(z,w)\in\mathscr{C}
if and only if
(\bar{z},\bar{w})\in\mathscr{C}
.
The curve
\mathscr{C}
is said to be
maximal
if the number of connected components of the real locus (in the compactified toric surface associated to the Newton polygon, see for example
[
61
]
) is equal to 2, that is equal to the genus plus 1.
The main result of this section, Theorem
25
below, identifies the values of the modular parameter
\tau
and of the angle map
\bm{\alpha}
corresponding to
real
curves. In the following section, we identify the values of the parameter
t
associated to
real dimer models
. These results generalize
[
12
, Theorem 34]
where we characterized the values of the parameters
\tau,\bm{\alpha}
corresponding to simple Harnack curves and the values of
t
associated to positive dimer edge-weights.

Theorem 25
.

Consider an algebraic curve
\mathscr{C}
of genus 1 satisfying
(\dagger)
and Fock’s corresponding dimer model on
\mathsf{G}

with modular parameter
\tau
, angle map
\bm{\alpha}
(both fixed by
\mathscr{C}
), and free parameter
t\in{\mathbb{C}}
. Suppose that
\mathscr{C}
contains a real point. Then, the algebraic curve
\mathscr{C}
is real if and only if, for generic values, the modular parameter
\tau
belongs to
i{\mathbb{R}}^{+}\cup\bigl\{\frac{1}{2}+i{\mathbb{R}}^{+}\,:\Im(\tau)\geq\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\bigr\}
, up to modular transformations of
\tau
. Moreover,

1.

when
\tau\in i{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
then, generically, the angle map
\bm{\alpha}=(\alpha_{\vec{T}})_{\vec{T}\in\vec{\mathscr{T}}_{1}}
takes values in
{\mathbb{R}}+\{0,\frac{\tau}{2}\}\penalty\ [\Lambda]
. Moreover, the curve
\mathscr{C}
is maximal,

2.

when
\tau\in\frac{1}{2}+i{\mathbb{R}}
with
\Im(\tau)\geq\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}
then, generically, either
the angle map
\bm{\alpha}
takes values in
{\mathbb{R}}\penalty\ [\Lambda]
, or the angle map
\bm{\alpha}
takes values in
\bigl\{0,\frac{1}{2}\bigr\}+i{\mathbb{R}}\penalty\ [\Lambda]
. In both cases, the curve
\mathscr{C}
is not maximal.

Proof.

Consider the birational parameterization
\Psi
of the curve given by (
30
). Following
[
27
, Chapter 11, Section 88]
, the curve
\mathscr{C}
is invariant by complex conjugation if and only if the real structure is preserved by
\Psi
, meaning that there exists an anti-holomorphic involution
\sigma(u)=a\bar{u}+b
of the torus
{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)
such that, for every
u\in{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)
,
\overline{\Psi(u)}=\Psi(\sigma(u))
. Du Val furthermore classifies these involutions, see also
[
4
, Table 1]
, and obtains the following conditions on the modular parameter
\tau
(assuming it is generic) up to modular transformations, and on the coefficients
a
and
b
:

\circ

\tau\in i{\mathbb{R}},\Im(\tau)\geq 1
,
a=1
and
b\in\{0,\frac{1}{2}\}
, or
a=-1
and
b\in\{0,\frac{\tau}{2}\}
.

\circ

\tau\in\frac{1}{2}+i{\mathbb{R}},\Im(\tau)\geq\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}
,
a\in\{-1,1\}
and
b=0
.

Proof of Point 1
. Suppose that
\tau\in i{\mathbb{R}},\Im(\tau)\geq 1
. Then
-\bar{\tau}=\tau
, and using Identity (
19
) we have

\overline{\theta_{1,1}(z,\tau)}=\theta_{1,1}(\bar{z},-\bar{\tau})=\theta_{1,1}(\bar{z},\tau).

Consider the case where
\sigma(u)=\bar{u}+b
, with
b\in\{0,\frac{1}{2}\}
. The condition

\overline{\Psi(u)}=\Psi(\sigma(u))
written for the first component reads

\displaystyle\forall\,u\in{\mathbb{T}}(\tau),\quad\bar{c}_{1}\cdot e^{-2i\pi\ell_{v}\cdot\bar{u}}\prod_{\vec{T}\in\vec{\mathscr{T}}_{1}}\theta_{1,1}(\bar{u}-\bar{\alpha}_{\vec{T}})^{-v_{\vec{T}}}=c_{1}\cdot e^{2i\pi\ell_{v}\cdot(\bar{u}+b)}\prod_{\vec{T}\in\vec{\mathscr{T}}_{1}}\theta_{1,1}(\bar{u}+b-\alpha_{\vec{T}})^{-v_{\vec{T}}}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\

\displaystyle\forall\,u\in{\mathbb{T}}(\tau),\quad e^{-4i\pi\ell_{v}\cdot u}\prod_{\vec{T}\in\vec{\mathscr{T}}_{1}}\left(\frac{\theta_{1,1}(u-\bar{\alpha}_{\vec{T}})}{\theta_{1,1}(u+b-\alpha_{\vec{T}})}\right)^{-v_{\vec{T}}}=e^{2i\pi\ell_{v}\cdot b}\frac{c_{1}}{\bar{c}_{1}}.

By Lemma
19
, the left hand side of the above equation is a meromorphic function of
{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)
, which we denote by
m=m(u)
. The condition
\overline{\Psi(u)}=\Psi(\sigma(u))
is equivalent to asking that this function
m
is equal to an explicit constant, namely
e^{2i\pi\ell_{v}\cdot b}c_{1}/\bar{c}_{1}
. In particular, the function
m
needs to be constant, which is true if and only if the zeros of the numerator and the denominator cancel out. Using that zeros of the function
\theta_{1,1}(\,\cdot\,)
are the points
0\,[\Lambda]
, and using that by our genericity assumption on the angle map
\bm{\alpha}
, there are no relations between angles of distinct train-tracks, we deduce that the meromorphic function
m
is constant if and only if, for every train-track
\vec{T}
such that
v_{\vec{T}}\neq 0
,

\bar{\alpha}_{\vec{T}}=(\alpha_{\vec{T}}-b)\,[\Lambda]\Leftrightarrow\ 2i\Im(\alpha_{\vec{T}})=b\,[\Lambda]\ \Leftrightarrow\ b=0\text{ and }\Im(\alpha_{\vec{T}})\in\Bigl\{0,\frac{\tau}{2}\Bigr\}+\tau{\mathbb{Z}}.

The explicit value of
\frac{c_{1}}{\bar{c}_{1}}
can be derived by the evaluation of
m
at
u=0
. Since this is not needed in the sequel, we do note make this computation explicit.
Repeating the argument for the second component of the function
\Psi
, we deduce that the angle map
\bm{\alpha}
takes values in
{\mathbb{R}}+\bigl\{0,\frac{\tau}{2}\bigr\}\penalty\ [\Lambda]
. Note that in this part of the argument we have only used that
\tau\in i{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
, but not that
\Im(\tau)\geq 1
.

Now consider the case where
\sigma(u)=-\bar{u}+b
, with
b\in\{0,\frac{\tau}{2}\}
. The condition

\overline{\Psi(u)}=\Psi(\sigma(u))
written for the first component reads

\displaystyle\forall\,u\in{\mathbb{T}}(\tau),\quad\bar{c}_{1}\cdot e^{-2i\pi\ell_{v}\cdot\bar{u}}\prod_{\vec{T}\in\vec{\mathscr{T}}_{1}}\theta_{1,1}(\bar{u}-\bar{\alpha}_{\vec{T}})^{-v_{\vec{T}}}=c_{1}\cdot e^{2i\pi\ell_{v}\cdot(-\bar{u}+b)}\prod_{\vec{T}\in\vec{\mathscr{T}}_{1}}\theta_{1,1}(-\bar{u}+b-\alpha_{\vec{T}})^{-v_{\vec{T}}}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\

\displaystyle\forall\,u\in{\mathbb{T}}(\tau),\quad\prod_{\vec{T}\in\vec{\mathscr{T}}_{1}}\left(\frac{\theta_{1,1}(u-\bar{\alpha}_{\vec{T}})}{\theta_{1,1}(-u+b-\alpha_{\vec{T}})}\right)^{-v_{\vec{T}}}=e^{2i\pi\ell_{v}\cdot b}\frac{c_{1}}{\bar{c}_{1}}.

Using again that, by Lemma
19
, the left hand side of the above equation is a meromorphic function of
{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)
, we deduce that, for every train-track
\vec{T}
such that
v_{\vec{T}}\neq 0
, we must have the following condition:

\bar{\alpha}_{\vec{T}}=(-\alpha_{\vec{T}}+b)\,[\Lambda]\Leftrightarrow\ 2\Re(\alpha_{\vec{T}})=b\,[\Lambda]\ \Leftrightarrow\ b=0\text{ and }\Re(\alpha_{\vec{T}})\in\Bigl\{0,\frac{1}{2}\Bigr\}+{\mathbb{Z}}.

The evaluation of the left hand side at
u=0
allows to compute the ratio
\frac{c_{1}}{\bar{c}_{1}}
in this case.
Repeating the argument for the second component of the function
\Psi
,
we deduce that the angle map
\bm{\alpha}
takes values in
\bigl\{0,\frac{1}{2}\bigr\}+\tau{\mathbb{R}}\penalty\ [\Lambda].

Let us now consider the modular transformation
\tau_{1}=-\frac{1}{\tau}
, then the range
\Im(\tau)\geq 1
gets mapped to the range
\Im(\tau)\leq 1
. By Equation (
26
) giving the transformation of
\theta_{1,1}
under
\tau_{1}
, we obtain that the first component of the map
\Psi
can be written as:

z(u,\tau)=c_{1}\cdot e^{2i\pi\ell_{v}\cdot u}\prod_{\vec{T}\in\vec{\mathscr{T}}_{1}}\theta_{1,1}(u-\alpha_{\vec{T}},\tau)^{-v_{\vec{T}}}=\tilde{c}_{1}\prod_{\vec{T}\in\vec{\mathscr{T}}_{1}}\theta_{1,1}(\tau_{1}u-\tau_{1}\alpha_{\vec{T}},\tau_{1})^{-v_{\vec{T}}},

for some explicit constant
\tilde{c}_{1}
.
This implies that the range
\bigl\{0,\frac{1}{2}\bigr\}+\tau{\mathbb{R}}\penalty\ [\Lambda]
of the angle map
\bm{\alpha}
gets mapped to the range
\tau_{1}\bigl(\bigl\{0,\frac{1}{2}\bigr\}+\tau{\mathbb{R}}\bigr)={\mathbb{R}}+\bigl\{0,\frac{\tau_{1}}{2}\bigr\}\penalty\ [\Lambda]
. We recover the case of the involution
\sigma(u)=\bar{u}
in the range
\Im(\tau)\leq 1
. This proves that considering the two anti-holomorphic involutions
\sigma(u)=\bar{u}
, and
\sigma(u)=-\bar{u}
in the range
\tau\in i{\mathbb{R}}^{+},\Im(\tau)\geq 1
is equivalent to considering only the involution
\sigma(u)=\bar{u}
in the full range
\tau\in i{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
, thus ending the proof of Point 1. apart from maximality which is postponed below.

Proof of Point 2
. Suppose that
\tau\in\frac{1}{2}+i{\mathbb{R}}
with
\Im(\tau)\geq\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}
. Then
-\bar{\tau}=\tau-1
, and using Identities (
19
) and (
27
) we have

\overline{\theta_{1,1}(z,\tau)}=\theta_{1,1}(\bar{z},\tau-1)=e^{-i\frac{\pi}{4}}\theta_{1,1}(\bar{z},\tau).

Consider first the case where
\sigma(u)=\bar{u}
. The Condition
\overline{\Psi(u)}=\Psi(\sigma(u))
written for the first component is:

\displaystyle\forall\,u\in{\mathbb{T}}(\tau),\quad e^{-4i\pi\ell_{v}\cdot u}\prod_{\vec{T}\in\vec{\mathscr{T}}_{1}}\left(\frac{\theta_{1,1}(u-\bar{\alpha}_{\vec{T}})}{\theta_{1,1}(u-\alpha_{\vec{T}})}\right)^{-v_{\vec{T}}}=\frac{c_{1}}{\bar{c}_{1}}.

The same argument as in the case where
\tau\in i{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
gives us that
2i\Im(\alpha_{\vec{T}})=0\,[\Lambda]
, but this time, since
\tau=\frac{1}{2}+i{\mathbb{R}}
, the only solution is
\Im(\alpha_{\vec{T}})\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Repeating the argument for the second component, we deduce that the angle map
\bm{\alpha}
takes values in
{\mathbb{R}}\penalty\ [\Lambda]
.

Lastly, consider the case where
\sigma(u)=-\bar{u}
. Then, arguing in a similar way, we obtain that
2\Re(\alpha_{\vec{T}})=0\,[\Lambda]
, so that
\Re(\alpha_{\vec{T}})\in\bigl\{0,\frac{1}{2}\bigr\}+{\mathbb{Z}}
, which yields
\alpha_{\vec{T}}\in\bigl\{0,\frac{1}{2}\bigr\}+i{\mathbb{R}}\penalty\ [\Lambda]
.

Note that we have not used the assumption
\Im(\tau)\geq\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}
. It is here to signify that, up to modular transformations, we can restrict to the standard fundamental domain of the modular parameter
\tau
.

Study of maximality of the curve
\mathscr{C}
.
Given the explicit parameterization
\Psi
, the real locus of
\mathscr{C}
is given by the image by
\Psi
of the points
\{u\in{\mathbb{T}}(\tau):\,\Psi(u)=\overline{\Psi(u)}\}
.
Since the anti-holomorphic involution
\sigma
commutes with complex conjugation,
i.e.

\overline{\Psi(u)}=\Psi(\sigma(u))
, the real locus is given by the image of the set of points of
{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)
such that
\sigma(u)=u
.
In Point 1, this set consists of the image of the two components
{\mathbb{R}}+\{0,\frac{\tau}{2}\}\penalty\ [\Lambda]
.
Since the map
\Psi
is bi-rational, this image has two components and the curve
\mathscr{C}
is maximal.
In Point 2, when
\sigma(u)=\bar{u}
, then the real locus is the image of
{\mathbb{R}}\penalty\ [\Lambda]
, which consists of a single component and the curve is not maximal;
when
\sigma(u)=-\bar{u}
, then the real locus is the image of
\{0,\frac{1}{2}\}+i{\mathbb{R}}\penalty\ [\Lambda]
, which again consists of a single component and the curve is not maximal.
∎

Remark 26
.

1.

From the proof of Theorem
25
, we gather additional information on the real locus of the curve
\mathscr{C}
. In all cases, the real locus of
\mathscr{C}
is the image by
\Psi
of the
real locus of
{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)
defined to be the set of points that are invariant by the anti-holomorphic involution
\sigma
. More specifically, following the case handling of Theorem
25
, we have

(a)

The anti-holomorphic involution
\sigma
of
{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)
is given by
\sigma(u)=\bar{u}
, and the real locus of
{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)
is
{\mathbb{R}}+\{0,\frac{\tau}{2}\}\penalty\ [\Lambda]
.

(b)

The anti-holomorphic involution
\sigma
of
{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)
is either given by
\sigma(u)=\bar{u}
in which case the real locus of
{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)
is
{\mathbb{R}}\penalty\ [\Lambda]
; or it is given by
\sigma(u)=-\bar{u}
in which case the real locus of
{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)
is
\{0,\frac{1}{2}\}+i{\mathbb{R}}\penalty\ [\Lambda]
.

2.

From the proof of Theorem
25
, we also know that if the modular parameter
\tau
satisfies the conditions of either Point 1. or Point 2., then
for all
angle maps
\bm{\alpha}
satisfying the respective conditions, the corresponding spectral curve has genus 1 and is invariant by complex conjugation; but the curve
\mathscr{C}
might be singular in general. The genericity assumption on the angle map is related to the curve being non singular.

3.3
Fock’s real dimer models

Consider an infinite, periodic minimal graph
\mathsf{G}
, and Fock’s dimer model on
\mathsf{G}
, with parameters
\tau,\bm{\alpha},t
, see Definition
21
. Assume that Fock’s dimer model is
real
, see Definition
2
, recalling that this means that it is gauge equivalent to a dimer model with
real weights
.

Then as a consequence of gauge equivalence, up to a scale change
(z,w)\leftrightarrow(\lambda z,\mu w)
, the spectral curve of Fock’s dimer model arises from a polynomial with real coefficients implying that, up to a scale change, it is invariant by complex conjugation, see Section
3.2
. We thus have that the modular parameter
\tau
and the angle map
\bm{\alpha}
must satisfy the conditions of Theorem
25
. Recall that this theorem gives no restriction on the parameter
t
; Theorem
27
below identifies the values of the parameter
t
corresponding to real dimer models.

Theorem 27
.

Consider Fock’s dimer model on an infinite, periodic minimal graph
\mathsf{G}
with
modular parameter
\tau
and angle map
\bm{\alpha}
as in the statement of Theorem
25
. Then, up to modular transformations of
\tau
,

1.

when
\tau\in i{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
, Fock’s dimer model is real for every generic angle map
\bm{\alpha}
taking values in
{\mathbb{R}}+\{0,\frac{\tau}{2}\}\penalty\ [\Lambda]
, if and only if the parameter
t
belongs to the real locus
{\mathbb{R}}+\{0,\frac{\tau}{2}\}\penalty\ [\Lambda]
of the torus
{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)
.

2.

when
\tau\in\frac{1}{2}+i{\mathbb{R}}
with
\Im(\tau)\geq\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}
,

\circ

the condition “Fock’s dimer model is real for every generic angle map
\bm{\alpha}
taking values in
{\mathbb{R}}\penalty\ [\Lambda]
” is never satisfied.

\circ

Fock’s dimer model is real for every generic angle map
\bm{\alpha}
taking values in
\bigl\{0,\frac{1}{2}\bigr\}+i{\mathbb{R}}\penalty\ [\Lambda]
, if and only if
t\in\bigl\{\frac{1}{4},\frac{3}{4}\bigr\}+i{\mathbb{R}}\penalty\ [\Lambda]
.

Proof.

Fock’s dimer model is real if and only if, for every face
\mathsf{f}
of
\mathsf{G}
,

\overline{\mathscr{W}_{\bm{\alpha},t}(\mathsf{f})}=\mathscr{W}_{\bm{\alpha},t}(\mathsf{f}).

(32)

Proof of Point 1
. Suppose that
\tau\in i{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
, then by Equation (
19
),

\overline{\mathscr{W}_{\bm{\alpha},t}(\mathsf{f})}=\mathscr{W}_{\overline{\bm{\alpha}},\overline{t}}(\mathsf{f}).

Moreover for every
\bm{\alpha}
taking values in
\{0,\frac{\tau}{2}\}+{\mathbb{R}}\penalty\ [\Lambda]
, we have
\overline{\bm{\alpha}}=\bm{\alpha}\ [\Lambda]
. Recalling that by Lemma
22
, face-weights are meromorphic functions of the angle map
\bm{\alpha}
, we deduce that Condition (
32
) is true for every such
\bm{\alpha}
if and only if,
for every
\bm{\alpha}
taking values in
\{0,\frac{\tau}{2}\}+{\mathbb{R}}\penalty\ [\Lambda]
,

\frac{\mathscr{W}_{\bm{\alpha},\overline{t}}(\mathsf{f})}{\mathscr{W}_{\bm{\alpha},t}(\mathsf{f})}=1\ \Leftrightarrow\prod_{i=1}^{|\mathsf{f}|/2}\frac{\theta_{0,0}(\bar{t}+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{i}-\alpha_{i})}{\theta_{0,0}(\bar{t}+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{i+1}-\alpha_{i})}\frac{\theta_{0,0}(t+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{i+1}-\alpha_{i})}{\theta_{0,0}(t+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{i}-\alpha_{i})}=1.

(33)

Since the graph
\mathsf{G}
is minimal, train-tracks around a vertex are distinct
[
11
, Lemma 8]
, so that by assumption the angles
(\alpha_{i})
are generically independent.
The above condition thus implies that, for every
i\in\{1,\dots,|\mathsf{f}|/2\}
, for every
\alpha_{i}\in\{0,\frac{\tau}{2}\}+{\mathbb{R}}\penalty\ [\Lambda]
,

\frac{\theta_{0,0}(\bar{t}+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{i}-\alpha_{i})}{\theta_{0,0}(\bar{t}+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{i+1}-\alpha_{i})}\frac{\theta_{0,0}(t+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{i+1}-\alpha_{i})}{\theta_{0,0}(t+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{i}-\alpha_{i})}=c,

where the term
c
in the right hand side is constant when seen as a function of
\alpha_{i}
.

By Lemma
19
, the left hand side is a meromorphic function of
\alpha_{i}
on
{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)
which needs to be equal to a constant on the real component of
{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)
, consisting of two circles, hence it must be equal to a constant on the whole of
{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)
. This is true if and only if the zeros and the poles cancel. Using that zeros of the function
\theta_{0,0}
are located at
\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\tau}{2}\ [\Lambda]
, and using that the angle map
\bm{\alpha}
is generic, the above equation is true if and only if

\displaystyle\bar{t}+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{i}=t+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{i}\ [\Lambda],\text{ and }\bar{t}+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{i+1}=t+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{i+1}\ [\Lambda]

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\bar{t}=t\ [\Lambda]\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad 2i\Im(t)=0\ [\Lambda]\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad t\in{\mathbb{R}}+\Bigl\{0,\frac{\tau}{2}\Bigr\}\penalty\ [\Lambda].

When
t\in{\mathbb{R}}+\{0,\frac{\tau}{2}\}\penalty\ [\Lambda]
, we now know that the above ratio is constant, but we still need to check that Condition (
33
) holds, that is that the constant is indeed equal to 1. This can be checked explicitly using that
\bar{t}=t+r+s\tau
, with
r,s\in\{0,1\}
, using Identity (
20
) and the fact that the angles are fixed. This concludes the proof of Point 1.

Proof of Point 2
. Suppose that
\tau\in\frac{1}{2}+i{\mathbb{R}}
. Since the modular parameter will change in the forthcoming computations, we write its dependence in the face-weights. By
Equation (
19
),

\overline{\mathscr{W}_{\bm{\alpha},t}(\mathsf{f},\tau)}=\mathscr{W}_{\overline{\bm{\alpha}},\overline{t}}(\mathsf{f},-\bar{\tau})=\mathscr{W}_{\overline{\bm{\alpha}},\overline{t}}(\mathsf{f},\tau-1)=\mathscr{W}_{\overline{\bm{\alpha}},\overline{t}+\frac{1}{2}}(\mathsf{f},\tau),

where in the last equality we used the second equality of Lemma
24
.

\circ
Suppose first that the angle map
\bm{\alpha}
takes values in
{\mathbb{R}}\penalty\ [\Lambda]
, then we have
\overline{\bm{\alpha}}=\bm{\alpha}\ [\Lambda]
, so that Condition (
32
) is true for every such
\bm{\alpha}
if and only if, for every
\bm{\alpha}
taking values in
{\mathbb{R}}\penalty\ [\Lambda]
,

\frac{\mathscr{W}_{\bm{\alpha},\overline{t}+\frac{1}{2}}(\mathsf{f},\tau)}{\mathscr{W}_{\bm{\alpha},t}(\mathsf{f},\tau)}=1.

Arguing as in the case
\tau\in i{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
, this is true if and only if

\bar{t}+\frac{1}{2}=t\ [\Lambda]\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad 2i\Im(t)=\frac{1}{2}\ [\Lambda]\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad i\Im(t)=\frac{1}{4}\ [\Lambda/2].

This condition is never satisfied on the lattice
\Lambda/2
thus proving the first part of Point 2.

\circ
Suppose that the angle map
\bm{\alpha}
takes values in
\bigl\{0,\frac{1}{2}\bigr\}+i{\mathbb{R}}\penalty\ [\Lambda]
, then we have
\overline{\bm{\alpha}}=-\bm{\alpha}\ [\Lambda]
, so that Condition (
32
) is true for every such
\bm{\alpha}
if and only if, for every
\bm{\alpha}
taking values in
\bigl\{0,\frac{1}{2}\bigr\}+i{\mathbb{R}}\penalty\ [\Lambda]
,

\frac{\mathscr{W}_{-\bm{\alpha},\overline{t}+\frac{1}{2}}(\mathsf{f},\tau)}{\mathscr{W}_{\bm{\alpha},t}(\mathsf{f},\tau)}=1.

(34)

Since the computation is a bit more delicate, let us detail the next step. The above implies the following condition

\frac{\theta_{0,0}(\bar{t}+\frac{1}{2}-\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})-\beta_{i}+\alpha_{i})}{\theta_{0,0}(\bar{t}+\frac{1}{2}-\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})-\beta_{i+1}+\alpha_{i})}\frac{\theta_{0,0}(t+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{i+1}-\alpha_{i})}{\theta_{0,0}(t+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{i}-\alpha_{i})}=c,

which is equivalent to the following condition

\displaystyle-\bar{t}-\frac{1}{2}+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{i}=t+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{i}\ [\Lambda],\text{ and }-\bar{t}-\frac{1}{2}+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{i+1}=t+\mathbf{d}(\mathsf{f})+\beta_{i+1}\ [\Lambda]

\displaystyle\quad\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle-\bar{t}-\frac{1}{2}=t\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad 2\Re(t)=-\frac{1}{2}\ [\Lambda]\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\Re(t)=-\frac{1}{4}\ [\Lambda/2]\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad t\in\Bigl\{\frac{1}{4},\frac{3}{4}\Bigr\}+i{\mathbb{R}}\penalty\ [\Lambda].

The fact that the ratio (
34
) is indeed equal to 1 when
2\Re(t)=-\frac{1}{2}\ [\Lambda]
is checked similarly to Point 1. This ends the proof of the second part of Point 2.
∎

Remark 28
.

Similarly to Point 2. of Remark
26
, we gather from the proof of Theorem
27
that if the modular parameter
\tau
satisfies the conditions of either Point 1. or Point 2., then
for all
angle maps
\bm{\alpha}
and parameters
t
satisfying the respective conditions, Fock’s corresponding dimer model is real.

4
Classification of (frustrated) Ising models in genus 1

Consider an Ising model on an infinite, periodic, isoradial graph
G
with real coupling constants
{\varepsilon}\mathsf{J}
, where the definition of isoradial graph is recalled in Section
3.1.1
.
Consider the corresponding Ising-dimer model on the infinite, periodic graph
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
, see Definition
11
. Then, by Lemma
31
of the forthcoming Section
4.1.1
, the graph
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
is minimal. Let
\mathscr{C}
be the spectral curve of this dimer model; assume that it is irreducible and has genus 1.
Suppose that a vertex of
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
is distinguished, allowing to define the spectral data of this Ising-dimer model on
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}

[
50
,
41
,
39
]
. Then, by Fock
[
32
]
, there exists a choice of modular parameter
\tau
, a periodic angle map
\bm{\alpha}
, and a parameter
t\in{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)
, such that the dimer model with Fock’s weights on
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
, see Definition
21
, is gauge equivalent to the Ising-dimer model on
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
with the given spectral data. The goal of this section is to characterize, and classify the Ising-dimer models on
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
through the set of parameters
\tau,\bm{\alpha},t
of Fock’s gauge equivalent dimer model. This is in the same spirit as the work of George
[
40
]
with the following difference: George uses the spectral data approach of
[
51
,
41
]
, whereas we use Fock’s approach
[
32
]
, allowing to have a very concrete model in the end, amenable to further study. Also, we allow for
real
coupling constants, and not only positive ones as in
[
40
]
, thus exiting the well behaved world of Harnack curves; but we restrict to genus 1 while
[
40
]
considers general genus spectral curves.

Before outlining the content of this section, let us summarize the information that we can gather on Fock’s gauge equivalent dimer model on
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
with parameters
\tau,\bm{\alpha},t
, using Section
3
. Since the curve
\mathscr{C}
arises from a polynomial with real coefficients, the curve is invariant by complex conjugation implying that the parameters
\tau,\bm{\alpha}
satisfy the conditions of Theorem
25
. The fact that
\mathscr{C}
arises from a polynomial with real coefficients also implies that Fock’s gauge equivalent dimer model is real, see Definition
2
, implying that the parameter
t
satisfies the conditions of Theorem
27
. This motivates the following.

Definition 29
.

We say that the parameters
\tau,\bm{\alpha},t
of Fock’s dimer model on
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
satisfy

Necessary Conditions I
if, up to modular transformations of
\tau
:

1.

either, the modular parameter
\tau\in i{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
, in which case the angle map
\bm{\alpha}
takes values in
{\mathbb{R}}+\{0,\frac{\tau}{2}\}\penalty\ [\Lambda]
, and the parameter
t
belongs to
{\mathbb{R}}+\{0,\frac{\tau}{2}\}\penalty\ [\Lambda]
,

2.

or, the modular parameter
\tau\in\frac{1}{2}+i{\mathbb{R}}
with
\Im(\tau)>\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}
, in which case the angle map
\bm{\alpha}
takes values in
\bigl\{0,\frac{1}{2}\bigr\}+i{\mathbb{R}}\penalty\ [\Lambda]
, and the parameter

t\in\bigl\{\frac{1}{4},\frac{3}{4}\bigr\}+i{\mathbb{R}}\penalty\ [\Lambda]
.

Recall that, by Points 2. of Remarks
26
and
28
, we do not need to assume that the angle map
\bm{\alpha}
is generic in order to have a spectral curve
\mathscr{C}
that it is invariant by complex conjugation, and such that Fock’s dimer model is real. This justifies that we take
all
angle maps taking values in the respective real loci of the torus
{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)
.

Remark 30
.

1.

Suppose that the Ising model is defined on an infinite, periodic, planar graph
G
(not necessarily isoradial), and consider its corresponding Ising-dimer model on
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
with spectral curve
\mathscr{C}
. Then, there exists a dimer model with Fock’s weights on an infinite, periodic,
minimal
graph
\mathsf{G}
which has the same spectral curve
\mathscr{C}
, up to a global scaling; but this time the graph
\mathsf{G}
cannot easily be related to the graph
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
.

2.

Recall that by Lemma
24
, we know that Fock’s dimer model is invariant under modular transformations of the modular parameter
\tau
, up to an appropriate transformation of the angle map
\bm{\alpha}
and of the parameter
t
. As a consequence, we only need to understand the model when the modular parameter is restricted to the appropriate subset of the standard fundamental domain, see Equation (
25
).

With Definition
29
at hand, we now turn to the content of this section.
By Point 2. of Remark
15
, we moreover know that the spectral curve
\mathscr{C}
of the Ising-dimer model on
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
is
centrally symmetric
,
i.e.
,
(z,w)\in\mathscr{C}
if and only if
\bigl(\frac{1}{z},\frac{1}{w}\bigr)\in\mathscr{C}
. This property has further implications on the angle map
\bm{\alpha}
, which is the subject of Theorem
32
of Section
4.1
, leading to Necessary Conditions II. Note that Necessary Conditions I and II are motivated by models defined on infinite, periodic, minimal graphs, but they still make sense when the graph
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
is not periodic. Hence, when not needed, we will not make the periodicity assumption. In Lemma
36
we explicitly compute face-weights of Fock’s dimer model on infinite, minimal graphs
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
for the three kinds of faces. In Lemma
37
, we prove a duality property for Fock’s dimer model.

Fock’s dimer model and the Ising-dimer model on an infinite, minimal graph
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
are gauge equivalent if and only if their face-weights are equal at all faces. Assuming that the parameters
\tau,\bm{\alpha},t
of Fock’s dimer model satisfy Necessary Conditions II, Sections
4.2
to
4.4
aim at characterizing the parameters
\tau,\bm{\alpha},t
that arise from gauge equivalent Ising-dimer models on
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
. More precisely, we have the following.
Recall that faces of
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
are of three kinds: faces corresponding to squares
y=\{e,e^{*}\}
, where
e,e^{*}
is a pair of primal/dual edge of
G/G^{*}
, those corresponding to vertices
v
of
G
, and those corresponding to dual vertices
f
of
G^{*}
.
The subject of Section
4.2
is to see the implications of equality of face-weights at square faces
y=\{e,e^{*}\}
:

\mathscr{W}^{\scriptsize{\mathrm{I}}}_{{\varepsilon}\mathsf{J}}(y)=\mathscr{W}_{\bm{\alpha},t}(y).

(35)

In particular
\mathscr{W}_{\bm{\alpha},t}(y)
must be negative since
\mathscr{W}^{\scriptsize{\mathrm{I}}}_{{\varepsilon}\mathsf{J}}(y)
is, see Equation (
13
).
The first result is Proposition
39
proving that necessarily
\tau\in i{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
. An important implication of this, see Corollary
40
, is that when the graph
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
is periodic, the spectral curve
\mathscr{C}
is
maximal
.
Then, in Proposition
41
, we derive a local condition necessarily satisfied by the angle map
\bm{\alpha}
and the parameter
t
, and turn it into a global condition in Proposition
43
.
This leads to the definition of Necessary Conditions III, and of the absolute value coupling constants
\mathsf{J}_{(\bm{\alpha},\rho,t)}
satisfying (
35
).

In Section
4.3
, we study implications of equality of
squared
face-weights at faces corresponding to vertices
v
of
G
and dual vertices
f
of
G^{*}
:

\forall\,f\in V^{*},\ v\in V,\quad(\mathscr{W}^{\scriptsize{\mathrm{I}}}_{{\varepsilon}\mathsf{J}_{(\bm{\alpha},\rho,t)}})^{2}(f)=(\mathscr{W}_{\bm{\alpha},t})^{2}(f),\quad(\mathscr{W}^{\scriptsize{\mathrm{I}}}_{{\varepsilon}\mathsf{J}_{(\bm{\alpha},\rho,t)}})^{2}(v)=(\mathscr{W}_{\bm{\alpha},t})^{2}(v).

This leads to further conditions on the angle map
\bm{\alpha}
and the parameter
t
, which is the content of Proposition
46
and the subject of Necessary Conditions IV.
Face-weights satisfying these conditions are computed in Lemma
49
using Jacobi elliptic functions.

In Proposition
50
of Section
4.4
, we explore a last condition on the angle map
\bm{\alpha}
needed to have equality of
face-weights
, and not only of
squared face-weights
, leading to the definition of Necessary Conditions V. This characterization of parameters
\tau,\bm{\alpha},t
of Fock’s dimer model arising from gauge equivalent Ising-dimer models on
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
allows us to prove the main result of this paper, namely
Theorem
52
of Section
4.5
, classifying Ising models; we then define the notion of algebraic phase transition, infer algebraic critical temperatures, and explain the relations to the corresponding notions arising from physics.

4.1
Centrally symmetric dimer models on
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
with real edge-weights

Consider the Ising-dimer model on an infinite, periodic, minimal graph
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
, with periodic coupling constants
{\varepsilon}\mathsf{J}
. Let
\mathscr{C}
be the associated spectral curve, assume that it has genus 1 and satisfies Assumptions
(\dagger)
. Then by Point 2. of Remark
15
, we know that this curve is centrally symmetric. Consider also Fock’s gauge equivalent dimer model on
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
with parameters
\tau,\bm{\alpha},t
, thus satisfying Necessary Conditions I. The main result of this section is Theorem
32
of Section
4.1.2
identifying the values of the angle map
\bm{\alpha}
corresponding to curves that have the property of being centrally symmetric, leading to Necessary Conditions II. We start with Section
4.1.1
, highlighting useful properties of train-tracks of the graph
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
. Finally, in Lemma
36
of Section
4.1.3
, we explicitly compute face-weights of Fock’s dimer model on
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
when it satisfies Necessary Conditions I and II.

4.1.1
Train-tracks of the graph
G
and of the graph
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}

Train-tracks of
G
and of
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
, and angle map.

Let
G
be an infinite, planar graph as in Section
3.1.1
. Recall that
G^{\diamond}
is the associated quad-graph, and that
\mathscr{T}
is the set of train-tracks, consisting of paths in the dual graph
(G^{\diamond})^{*}
crossing opposite sides of quadrangles. A priori, there is no natural consistent orientation of the train-tracks of
\mathscr{T}
, see Figure
4
(left).

\begin{overpic}[fig_tt_G_GQ.pdf]
\end{overpic}

Figure 4
:
Comparison of the set of train-tracks
\mathscr{T}
(left),
\vec{\mathscr{T}}_{0}
(center) and
\vec{\mathscr{T}}
(right).

Consider the graph
\mathsf{G}^{\diamond\scriptscriptstyle{/2}}_{0}
obtained from
\mathsf{G}^{\diamond}
by cutting all quadrangles into four. The corresponding set of train-tracks can be consistently oriented so that vertices of
G
and
G^{*}
are on the right, see Figure
4
(center), and we denote this set by
\vec{\mathscr{T}}_{0}
. To each train-track
T
of
\mathscr{T}
, there corresponds a pair
\{\vec{T},\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}\}
of non-intersecting train-tracks of
\vec{\mathscr{T}}_{0}
, oriented in opposite directions. As a consequence, two oriented train-tracks of
\vec{\mathscr{T}}_{0}
intersect if and only of they arise from distinct intersecting train-tracks of
\mathscr{T}
.

Now consider the graph
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
, and the associated quad-graph
\mathsf{G}^{\diamond\scriptscriptstyle{/2}}
; there are two kinds of quadrangles: those arising from quadrangles of
\mathsf{G}^{\diamond\scriptscriptstyle{/2}}_{0}
, and “flat” quadrangles corresponding to external edges of
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
, see Figure
4
(right). Since the graph
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
is bipartite, train-tracks are consistently oriented so that white vertices of
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
are on the left, see Section
3.1.1
, and this set is denoted by
\vec{\mathscr{T}}
. The set of train-tracks
\vec{\mathscr{T}}
is obtained from
\vec{\mathscr{T}}_{0}
by adding a crossing between train-tracks of a pair at each “flat” quadrangle, see Figure
4
(center and right). As a consequence, no train-track is deleted or created, the only new intersections are between train-tracks of a pair, and they arise in opposite direction. We thus have that, to every (unoriented) train-track
T
of
\mathscr{T}
, there corresponds a pair
\{\vec{T}
,
\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}\}
of oriented train-tracks of
\vec{\mathscr{T}}
. Note that, without further assumption on
G
, there is no consistent way of assigning arrows to the pair
\{\vec{T},\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}\}
just by looking at
T
; we nevertheless use the notation
\vec{T},\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}
implying that a choice of labeling has been made, this is possible but this labeling does not come from an information we have on
T
. The relation between
G
and
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
implies the following.

Lemma 31
.

The graph
G
is isoradial if and only if the graph
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
is minimal.

Proof.

Returning to the construction of the set of oriented train-tracks
\vec{\mathscr{T}}
from
\mathscr{T}
, we have that train-tracks self intersect in
\mathscr{T}
if and only of they do so in
\vec{\mathscr{T}}
. We also have that two distinct train-tracks of
\mathscr{T}
intersect more than once, if and only if two distinct oriented train-tracks of
\vec{\mathscr{T}}
arising from a distinct pair of train-tracks of
\mathscr{T}
intersect in the same direction, which is forbidden in minimal graphs. The only additional crossings that we have in
\vec{\mathscr{T}}
and not in
\mathscr{T}
come from oriented train-tracks of a pair, which intersect in the opposite direction, and this is allowed in minimal graphs.
∎

Consider a train-track
T
of
\mathscr{T}
, and a choice of labeling
\vec{T},\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}
for the two corresponding oriented train-tracks of
\vec{\mathscr{T}}
. Then, the angle map
\bm{\alpha}
assigns angles
\alpha_{\vec{T}}
and
\alpha_{\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}}
to the train-tracks
\vec{T},\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}
.
When the graph
G
is furthermore periodic, let us denote by
\mathscr{T}_{1}
the set of train-tracks of
G_{1}
, and by
\vec{\mathscr{T}}_{1}
the set of oriented train-tracks of
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}_{1}
. Observe that the homology classes of train-tracks of

\vec{\mathscr{T}}_{1}
come in opposite pairs: for every
T\in\mathscr{T}_{1}
,
(h_{\vec{T}},v_{\vec{T}})=-(h_{\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}},v_{\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}})
.

4.1.2
Centrally symmetric dimer models on
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}

Consider the Ising-dimer model on an infinite, periodic, minimal graph
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
with associated
spectral curve
\mathscr{C}
of genus 1 satisfying Assumptions
(\dagger)
. Consider Fock’s gauge equivalent dimer model with parameters
\tau,\bm{\alpha},t
satisfying Necessary Conditions I. Then the spectral curve
\mathscr{C}
and that of Fock’s dimer model are related by a global scaling
(z,w)\leftrightarrow(\lambda z,\mu w)
, with
(\lambda,\mu)\in({\mathbb{C}}^{*})^{2}
, see Point 2. of Remark
5
. A bi-rational parameterization of
\mathscr{C}
is given by the map
\Psi
of Equation (
30
) on the graph
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
, with
c_{1},c_{2}\in{\mathbb{C}}
. Using the specific structure of the train-tracks exhibited in Section
4.1.1
, we suppose that a labeling
\vec{T},\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}
of the pairs of train-tracks is chosen. The map
\Psi:{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
can then further be written as
\Psi(u)=(z(u),w(u))
, with
z,w
given by

z(u)=c_{1}\cdot e^{2i\pi\ell_{v}\cdot u}\prod_{T\in\mathscr{T}_{1}}\left(\frac{\theta_{1,1}(u-\alpha_{\vec{T}})}{\theta_{1,1}(u-\alpha_{\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}})}\right)^{-v_{\vec{T}}},\hskip 9.24994ptw(u)=c_{2}\cdot e^{-2i\pi\ell_{h}\cdot u}\prod_{T\in\mathscr{T}_{1}}\left(\frac{\theta_{1,1}(u-\alpha_{\vec{T}})}{\theta_{1,1}(u-\alpha_{\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}})}\right)^{h_{\vec{T}}},

(36)

for some
c_{1},c_{2}\in{\mathbb{C}}
, where

\sum_{\vec{T}\in\vec{\mathscr{T}}_{1}}v_{\vec{T}}(\alpha_{\vec{T}}-\alpha_{\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}})=\ell_{v}\tau,\quad\sum_{\vec{T}\in\vec{\mathscr{T}}_{1}}h_{\vec{T}}(\alpha_{\vec{T}}-\alpha_{\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}})=\ell_{h}\tau,

(37)

with
\ell_{v},\ell_{h}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Recall from Point 2. of Remark
15
that the curve
\mathscr{C}
has the additional property of being centrally symmetric. Since the curve
\mathscr{C}
is non-singular (apart for isolated real nodes), the angle map is generic in the sense that there are a priori no relations between angles of oriented train-tracks of
\vec{\mathscr{T}}
arising from distinct train-tracks of
\mathscr{T}
, as linear relations for example. Theorem
32
below identifies the values of the angle map
\bm{\alpha}
of Fock’s gauge equivalent dimer model when the curve
\mathscr{C}
has this additional property.

Theorem 32
.

Consider the Ising-dimer model on an infinite, periodic minimal graph
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
with periodic real edge-weights and genus 1 spectral curve
\mathscr{C}
satisfying
(\dagger)
. Consider Fock’s gauge equivalent dimer model with parameters
\tau
, generic angle map
\bm{\alpha}
,
t
satisfying Necessary Conditions I. Then, the spectral curve
\mathscr{C}
is centrally symmetric if and only if, up to modular transformations of the modular parameter
\tau
, for every train-track
T
of
\mathscr{T}_{1}
,

1.

when
\tau\in i{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
, then

\alpha_{\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}}=\alpha_{\vec{T}}+\rho\ [\Lambda]
,
for some half-period vector
\rho=\frac{j}{2}+\frac{\ell}{2}\tau,\ (j,\ell)\in\{0,1\}^{2}
, with
(j,\ell)\neq(0,0)
,

2.

when
\tau\in\frac{1}{2}+i{\mathbb{R}}
, with
\Im(\tau)>\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}
, then
\alpha_{\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}}=\alpha_{\vec{T}}+\rho\ [\Lambda]
, with
\rho=\frac{1}{2}
.

Proof.

Consider the birational parameterization
\Psi
of the spectral curve
\mathscr{C}
given by (
36
). By central symmetry of the curve
\mathscr{C}
, we have that

(z,w)\in\mathscr{C}
if and only if
\bigl(\frac{1}{z},\frac{1}{w}\bigr)\in\mathscr{C}
, implying that
\frac{1}{\Psi(u)}:=\bigl(\frac{1}{z(u)},\frac{1}{w(u)}\bigr)
yields another parameterization of the curve
\mathscr{C}
. By
[
27
, Corollary, Section 86]
, this means
that there exists a holomorphic automorphism
\sigma
of the torus
{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)
such that
1/\Psi(u)=\Psi(\sigma(u))
. The further condition
1/(1/\Psi(u))=\Psi(u)
implies that
\sigma
must be an involution. The condition of being a holomorphic involution on
\sigma
implies that
\sigma=au+b
with
a=1
and
b\in\{0,\frac{1}{2},\frac{\tau}{2},\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\tau}{2}\}
, or
a=-1
and
b\in{\mathbb{C}}
.

\circ
Consider first the case where
a=1
and
b\in\{0,\frac{1}{2},\frac{\tau}{2},\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\tau}{2}\}
. Then, looking at the first component of the map
\Psi
, we must have
\frac{1}{z(u)}=z(u+b)
, that is

\displaystyle c_{1}^{-1}\cdot e^{-2i\pi\ell_{v}\cdot u}\prod_{T\in\mathscr{T}_{1}}\left(\frac{\theta_{1,1}(u-\alpha_{\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}})}{\theta_{1,1}(u-\alpha_{\vec{T}})}\right)^{-v_{\vec{T}}}=c_{1}e^{2i\pi\ell_{v}\cdot(u+b)}\prod_{T\in\mathscr{T}_{1}}\left(\frac{\theta_{1,1}(u+b-\alpha_{\vec{T}})}{\theta_{1,1}(u+b-\alpha_{\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}})}\right)^{-v_{\vec{T}}}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\,

\displaystyle e^{-4i\pi\ell_{v}\cdot u}\prod_{T\in\mathscr{T}_{1}}\left(\frac{\theta_{1,1}(u-\alpha_{\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}})}{\theta_{1,1}(u-\alpha_{\vec{T}})}\frac{\theta_{1,1}(u+b-\alpha_{\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}})}{\theta_{1,1}(u+b-\alpha_{\vec{T}})}\right)^{-v_{\vec{T}}}=(c_{1})^{2}\cdot e^{2i\pi\ell_{v}b}.

The argument then resembles that of the proof of Theorem
25
. By Lemma
19
, the left hand side is a meromorphic function on
{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)
which must be equal to a constant, namely
(c_{1})^{2}\cdot e^{2i\pi\ell_{v}b}
. This meromorphic function is equal to a constant if and only if the zeros of the numerator and denominator cancel out. Using that the zeros of the function
\theta_{1,1}(\,\cdot\,)
are the points
0\,[\Lambda]
, and using that by our genericity assumption on the angle map
\bm{\alpha}
there are no relations between pairs of oriented train-tracks
\vec{T},\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}
corresponding to distinct train-tracks
T
, we deduce that, for every train-track
T
such that
v_{\vec{T}}\neq 0
, we have

\circ

either
\alpha_{\vec{T}}=\alpha_{\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}}
, but then this cancels a zero and a pole of the function
z
, thus changing the number of zeros and poles of
z
, which is not possible,

\circ

or
\alpha_{\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}}=\alpha_{\vec{T}}-b\,[\Lambda],\text{ and }\alpha_{\vec{T}}=\alpha_{\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}}-b\,[\Lambda]
. When
b\in\{0,\frac{1}{2},\frac{\tau}{2},\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\tau}{2}\}
, the two conditions are compatible, and give

\alpha_{\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}}=\alpha_{\vec{T}}-b\,[\Lambda]
. For the same reason as above, we must exclude the case
b=0
.

We thus have
\alpha_{\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}}=\alpha_{\vec{T}}+\rho\ [\Lambda]
for some half-period vector
\rho
as in the statement, with
(j,\ell)\neq(0,0)
. The explicit value of the constant
(c_{1})^{2}\cdot e^{2i\pi\ell_{v}b}
can be computed by evaluating the meromorphic function at
u=0
. Since this is not needed in the sequel, we do not perform this computation. Repeating the argument for the second component of the map
\Psi
yields that this holds for all train-tracks.

We must now take into account the fact that the parameters
\tau,\bm{\alpha}
satisfy Necessary Condition I. When
\tau\in i{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
,

\bm{\alpha}
takes values in
{\mathbb{R}}+\{0,\frac{\tau}{2}\}\penalty\ [\Lambda]
, which is compatible with the condition
\alpha_{\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}}=\alpha_{\vec{T}}+\rho
, ending the proof of Point 1. When
\tau\in\frac{1}{2}+i{\mathbb{R}}
,
\bm{\alpha}
takes values in
\bigl\{0,\frac{1}{2}\bigr\}+i{\mathbb{R}}\penalty\ [\Lambda]
.
This condition is compatible with
\alpha_{\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}}=\alpha_{\vec{T}}+\rho\ [\Lambda]
if and only if
\ell=0
, thus ending the proof of Point 2.

\circ
Consider now the case where
a=-1
, and
b\in{\mathbb{C}}
. Since the map
(z,w)\mapsto(\frac{1}{z},\frac{1}{w})
preserves the real locus of the curve, and since the orientation is preserved on each of the real components,
the holomorphic involution
\sigma(u)=-u+b
, which is orientation reversing, cannot occur.
∎

Remark 33
.

Form the proof of Theorem
32
, we also gather that we do not need the angle map
\bm{\alpha}
to be generic in order for the spectral curve to have central symmetry; this holds for all angle maps satisfying the conditions of Point 1. or Point 2.

Theorem
32
and Remark
33
motivate the following.

Definition 34
.

We say that the parameters
\tau,\bm{\alpha},t
of Fock’s dimer model on
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
satisfy
Necessary Conditions II
, if they satisfy Necessary Conditions I and if, up to modular transformations of
\tau
,

1.

either
\tau\in i{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
, then
\alpha_{\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}}=\alpha_{\vec{T}}+\rho\ [\Lambda]
,
for some half-period vector
\rho=\frac{j}{2}+\frac{\ell}{2}\tau,\ (j,\ell)\in\{0,1\}^{2}
with
(j,\ell)\neq(0,0)
,

2.

or
\tau\in\frac{1}{2}+i{\mathbb{R}}
, with
\Im(\tau)>\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}
, then
\alpha_{\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}}=\alpha_{\vec{T}}+\rho\ [\Lambda]
, with
\rho=\frac{1}{2}
.

To emphasize the dependence in
\rho
, we now write the parameters of Fock’s dimer model as
\tau,\bm{\alpha},\rho,t
. Note that, as in Definition
29
, periodicity of the graph
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
motivates this definition but is not required for it to make sense.

4.1.3
Face-weights of Fock’s dimer model on
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
and duality

Consider Fock’s dimer model on an infinite minimal graph
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
with parameters
\tau,\bm{\alpha};\rho,t
satisfying Necessary Conditions II. In this section, we do not need to assume that the graph
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
is periodic; we explicitly compute face-weights. Instead of denoting them by
\mathscr{W}_{\bm{\alpha},t}(\,\cdot\,,\tau)=\mathscr{W}_{\bm{\alpha},t}(\,\cdot\,)
as in Section
3.1.2
, let us denote them by
\mathscr{W}_{\bm{\alpha},\rho,t}(\,\cdot\,,\tau)=\mathscr{W}_{\bm{\alpha},\rho,t}(\,\cdot\,)
emphasizing the dependence in
\rho
stemming from Necessary Conditions II.

Notation.

Recall that there is no natural way of globally assigning the arrows to the pair of oriented train-tracks
\vec{T},\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}
of
\vec{\mathscr{T}}
corresponding to a train-track
T
of
\mathscr{T}
, but locally around each face of
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
, a consistent choice can be made, justifying the upcoming notation. For the angles
\alpha_{\vec{T}}
,
\alpha_{\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}}
, when no reference to the train-tracks is needed, we will simply denote the pair of angles as
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}
.

Around a face corresponding to a dual vertex
f
of degree
|f|
of
G
, see Figure
5
(left), the angles
(\alpha_{i})
are associated to train-tracks turning clockwise around
f
, and then angles
(\beta_{i})
are associated to train-tracks turning counterclockwise, with
i\in\{1,\dots,|f|\}
, and the respective numbering being such that
\beta_{i+1}=\alpha_{i}^{\prime}
. Around a face corresponding to a vertex
v
of degree
|v|
of
G
, see Figure
5
(right), the angles
(\tilde{\alpha}_{i})
correspond to train-tracks turning counterclockwise, and the angles
(\tilde{\beta}_{i})
correspond to train-tracks turning clockwise, with
i\in\{1,\dots,|v|\}
, and respective labeling being such that
\tilde{\beta}_{i+1}=\tilde{\alpha}_{i}^{\prime}
.

:

f
of
G
. Right: notation around a face corresponding to a vertex
v
of
G
.

Around a face corresponding to a square
y=\{e,e^{*}\}
of
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
, we use
\alpha_{e},\beta_{e},\alpha_{e}^{\prime},\beta_{e}^{\prime}
as in Figure
6
(left), where the angles
\alpha_{e},\beta_{e}
, and
\alpha_{e}^{\prime},\beta_{e}^{\prime}
, are the angles of the two edges of the square corresponding to an edge
e
of
G
; we also use the notation
\tilde{\alpha}_{e}:=\alpha_{e^{*}}\,\tilde{\beta}_{e}=\beta_{e^{*}},\tilde{\alpha}^{\prime}_{e}=\alpha^{\prime}_{e^{*}},\tilde{\beta}^{\prime}_{e}=\beta^{\prime}_{e^{*}}
, where
\tilde{\alpha}_{e},\tilde{\beta}_{e}
, and
\tilde{\alpha}^{\prime}_{e},\tilde{\beta}^{\prime}_{e}
, are the angles of the two edges of the square corresponding to a dual edge
e^{*}
of
G^{*}
, see Figure
6
(right).
