\put(14.0,-2.0){\scriptsize$T_{0}$}
\put(44.0,9.0){\scriptsize$T_{0}$}
\put(44.0,14.0){\scriptsize$T_{1}$}
\put(35.0,43.0){\scriptsize$T_{1}$}
\put(26.0,43.0){\scriptsize$T_{2}$}
\put(-3.0,33.0){\scriptsize$T_{2}$}
\put(-3.0,28.0){\scriptsize$T_{3}$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 10
:
Left:
G
is the square lattice with a
2\times 2
fundamental domain; oriented train-tracks of
\mathscr{T}
are pictured in red. Right: the corresponding graph
{\mathsf{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}}}
; oriented train-tracks
\vec{T}
of
\mathscr{T}_{*,\circ}
, in orientation preserving bijection with those of
\mathscr{T}
, are pictured with full lines; oriented train-tracks

\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}

of
\mathscr{T}_{*,\bullet}
are pictured with dotted lines.

Proposition 69
.

Fock’s dimer model with parameters
k^{2}\in[0,1)
,
\rho=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\tau}{2}
, angle map

\alpha_{\vec{T}_{0}}=0,\alpha_{\vec{T}_{1}}=\frac{1}{4},\alpha_{\vec{T}_{2}}=\frac{1}{2},\alpha_{\vec{T}_{3}}=\frac{3}{4}
is gauge equivalent to the Ising-dimer model arising from the isotropic frustrated Ising model with coupling constants
{\varepsilon}\mathsf{J}
, given by

\sinh(2\mathsf{J})=\frac{\sqrt{k^{\prime}(1+k^{\prime})}}{k}.

As
k
runs from
0
to
1
, this parameterizes all possible coupling constants
\mathsf{J}\in(0,\infty)
.

Proof.

Since the angle map preserves the cyclic order of the train-tracks of
\mathscr{T}
, we have by Corollary
60
that this model corresponds to an Ising model, frustrated at every face.
Let us identify the coupling constants.
At every square face
y
, by Table
65
and using
[
56
, (2.4.10)]
,
we obtain

\begin{aligned} \sinh^{2}(2\mathsf{J})=\frac{(k^{\prime})^{2}}{k^{2}}\nc^{2}(K/2)=\frac{k^{\prime}(1+k^{\prime})}{k^{2}}\end{aligned}\qed

One can use this parameterization to plot the amoeba and real locus of the spectral curve, see Figure
11
(left and center).
At first sight, this does not seem to be coherent with our set of parameters.
On the one hand, looking at the amoeba and real locus, the curve seems to be Harnack.
On the other hand, the curve is parameterized by Equation (
36
) with the
\alpha_{\vec{T}_{i}},\alpha_{\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}_{i}}
pictured in Figure
11
(right).
Hence we would expect the amoeba to feature two superimposed “sheets” as in the right of Figure
1
.

\begin{overpic}[fig_rectangle_4.pdf]
\put(-4.0,42.0){\scriptsize$\alpha_{\vec{T}_{0}}$}
\put(23.0,42.0){\scriptsize$\alpha_{\vec{T}_{1}}$}
\put(47.0,42.0){\scriptsize$\alpha_{\vec{T}_{2}}$}
\put(73.0,42.0){\scriptsize$\alpha_{\vec{T}_{3}}$}
\put(47.0,80.0){\scriptsize$\alpha_{\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}_{0}}$}
\put(73.0,80.0){\scriptsize$\alpha_{\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}_{1}}$}
\put(-4.0,80.0){\scriptsize$\alpha_{\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle T\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle T\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}_{2}}$}
\put(23.0,80.0){\scriptsize$\alpha_{\vec{T}_{3}}$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 11
:
On the left: the amoeba of the spectral curve of the isotropic frustrated Ising model on the square lattice for some
0<\mathsf{J}<\infty
.
In the middle: the image of the real locus through the map
(z,w)\to(\log|z|,\log|w|)
. On the right: the torus and the angle map used in the parameterization of Fock’s dimer model and its spectral curve.

To solve this apparent contradiction, we must take a closer look at the parameterization. We prove the following.

Proposition 70
.

The spectral curve of the isotropic frustrated Ising model on the square lattice:

•

is reducible, in the sense that it is the superposition of two identical copies of the same curve
\mathscr{C}
. In particular, it does not satisfy
(\dagger)
,

•

is the symmetric via
(z,w)\to(z,-w)
of the spectral curve of an isotropic ferromagnetic Ising model on the square lattice (with a different coupling constant which can be written down explicitly),

•

each of the copy of the curve is thus a Harnack curve.

As a consequence, the free energy of the model is given by Proposition
56
.
By Remark
57
, the algebraic phase transition at
k=0
, is in fact a phase transition in the sense that the free energy has a singularity.
Thus by Proposition
69
, the isotropic frustrated Ising model on the square lattice has a phase transition at zero temperature.

Proof.

The proof consists in writing down explicitly the parameterization
\Psi=(z(u),w(u)):{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)\to\mathscr{C}
of the spectral curve given by Equation (
36
) and applying algebraic identities on theta functions. Using the values of the angle map
\bm{\alpha}
of Proposition
69
, we have

z(u)=\frac{\theta_{1,1}(u)\theta_{1,1}(u-\tau/2)\theta_{1,1}(u-3/4)\theta_{1,1}(u-3/4-\tau/2)}{\theta_{1,1}(u-1/2)\theta_{1,1}(u-1/2-\tau/2)\theta_{1,1}(u-1/4)\theta_{1,1}(u-1/4-\tau/2)}

and

w(u)=\frac{\theta_{1,1}(u)\theta_{1,1}(u-\tau/2)\theta_{1,1}(u-1/4)\theta_{1,1}(u-1/4-\tau/2)}{\theta_{1,1}(u-1/2)\theta_{1,1}(u-1/2-\tau/2)\theta_{1,1}(u-3/4)\theta_{1,1}(u-3/4-\tau/2)}.

Using Equation (
22
) and Equation (20.7.12) of
[
25
]
, this can be rewritten as

z(u)=\frac{\theta_{1,1}(u,\tau/2)\theta_{1,1}(u-3/4,\tau/2)}{\theta_{1,1}(u-1/2,\tau/2)\theta_{1,1}(u-1/4,\tau/2)}\quad;\quad w(u)=-\frac{\theta_{1,1}(u,\tau/2)\theta_{1,1}(u-1/4,\tau/2)}{\theta_{1,1}(u-1/2,\tau/2)\theta_{1,1}(u-3/4,\tau/2)}.

As the proof is very similar to Proposition
69
in the (easier)
1\times 1
periodic case, we let the reader check that, upon applying the symmetry
(z,w)\to(z,-w)
, this is the parameterization of the isotropic ferromagnetic Ising model on the square lattice with parameter
\tau/2
.
It it thus a Harnack curve.

This result can also be obtained by computing directly the characteristic polynomials.
Denote by
P_{i\times j}^{\pm}
, the characteristic polynomial of the isotropic frustrated (if
\pm=-
), resp. non-frustrated (if
\pm=+
), Ising model on the
i\times j
periodic, square lattice, with absolute value coupling constant
\mathsf{J}_{-}
, resp. coupling constants
\mathsf{J}_{+}
.
One can check by direct computation that for all
\mathsf{J}_{-}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, there exists
\mathsf{J}_{+}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{+},\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
such that

P_{2\times 2}^{-}(z,w)=\lambda P_{1\times 1}^{+}(z,-w)^{2}.

Again, this proves the results of Proposition
70
, without resorting to our theory.
∎

Remark 71
.

This parameterization is coherent with Figure
11
.
In fact, the map
\Psi
has a vertical period
\tau/2
: it is two-to-one and the two circles
{\mathbb{R}}
and
{\mathbb{R}}+\tau/2
containing the zeros and poles have the same image through the map, which is the outer boundary of the amoeba.
Moreover, the image of the circle
{\mathbb{R}}+\tau/4
is also real, thus implying that the real locus of
\mathscr{C}
has a bounded component.

Appendix A
Identities involving theta and elliptic functions

In this section we provide statements and proofs of identities involving theta and elliptic functions that we use, and that we could not find as such in the literature.

Lemma 72
.

For
m,n\in\{0,1\}
with indices understood mod 2, and
z,w\in{\mathbb{C}}
,

\begin{split}\theta_{1,1}^{2}(z)\theta_{0,0}^{2}(w)+&(-1)^{1+m+mn}\theta_{m,n}^{2}(z)\theta_{m+1,n+1}^{2}(w)=\\
&=(-1)^{n+mn}\theta_{m+1,n+1}(z+w)\theta_{m+1,n+1}(z-w)\theta_{m,n}^{2}(0).\end{split}

Proof.

The following is a compact form of Equations
[
56
, (1.4.19)-(1.4.22)-(1.4.29)]
,

\displaystyle\theta_{1,1}^{2}(z)\theta_{1,1}^{2}(w)+(-1)^{m+n+mn}\theta_{m,n}^{2}(z)\theta_{m,n}^{2}(w)=(-1)^{m+n+mn}\theta_{m,n}^{2}(z+w)\theta_{m,n}^{2}(z-w)\theta_{m,n}^{2}(0),

noting that when
(m,n)=(1,1)
the left-hand-side vanishes, and
\theta_{1,1}^{2}(0)=0
in the right-hand-side. Evaluating the equation at
w\mapsto w+\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\tau}{2}
gives:

\displaystyle\theta_{1,1}^{2}(z)\theta_{1,1}^{2}

\displaystyle\Bigl(w+\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\tau}{2}\Bigr)+(-1)^{m+n+mn}\theta_{m,n}^{2}(z)\theta_{m,n}^{2}\Bigl(w+\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\tau}{2}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{m+n+mn}\theta_{m,n}\Bigl(z+w+\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\tau}{2}\Bigr)\theta_{m,n}\Bigl(z-w-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\tau}{2}\Bigr)\theta_{m,n}^{2}(0).

Using Identities (
22
) and (
23
) with
(j,\ell)=(1,1)
, we obtain

\displaystyle(q^{-1/4}e^{-i\pi w})^{2}

\displaystyle(i)^{2\cdot 2}\theta_{1,1}^{2}(z)\theta_{0,0}^{2}(w)+(-1)^{m+n+mn}(q^{-1/4}e^{-i\pi w})^{2}(i)^{2(n+1)}\theta_{m,n}^{2}(z)\theta_{m+1,n+1}^{2}(w)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{m+n+mn}(q^{-1/4}e^{-i\pi(z+w)})(q^{-1/4}e^{i\pi(z-w)})i^{2(n+1)}(-1)^{n+1+n+mn}(-1)^{m+n+mn}

\displaystyle\quad\theta_{m+1,n+1}(z+w)\theta_{m+1,n+1}(z-w)\theta_{m,n}^{2}(0).

The result is obtained after simplification in the above.
∎

Lemma 73
.

[
63
, §6, p.24]

Let
a_{1},\dots,a_{r},b_{1},\dots,b_{r}\in{\mathbb{C}}
. Then, the function

f(u)=e^{-2i\pi\ell u}\prod_{i=1}^{r}\frac{\theta_{m,n}(u-a_{i})}{\theta_{m,n}(u-b_{i})},

is a meromorphic function on
{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)
if and only if
\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, and
\sum_{i=1}^{r}(a_{i}-b_{i})-\ell\tau\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Proof.

In
[
63
]
, the result is written in the case where
\sum_{i=1}^{r}(a_{i}-b_{i})\in{\mathbb{Z}}
with no occurrence of the prefactor. Since the function
f
is meromorphic on
{\mathbb{C}}
, we are left with checking periodicity on
{\mathbb{T}}(\tau)
. The function
f
is periodic if and only if, for every
j^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
,
f(u+j^{\prime}+\ell^{\prime}\tau)=f(u)
. By Equation (
20
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{f(u+j^{\prime}+\ell^{\prime}\tau)}{f(u)}=e^{-2i\pi\ell(j^{\prime}+\ell^{\prime}\tau)}e^{-2i\pi\ell^{\prime}(\sum_{i=1}^{r}(-a_{i}+b_{i}))}.

This ratio is equal to 1 for every
j^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
if and only if
\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, and
\ell\tau+\sum_{i=1}^{r}(b_{i}-a_{i})\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, thus concluding the proof.
∎

Ascending Landen transformation and modular transformation of theta and Jacobi elliptic functions.

In this paragraph, we prove the following algebraic identities on Jacobi elliptic functions and theta functions.
Recall from Section
4.3.2
the notation
\gamma^{k}=2K(k)\gamma
.

Lemma 74
.

For
\tau\in{\mathbb{C}}
, let
\tau_{1}=2\tau
,
\tau_{2}=\tau_{1}-1=2\tau-1
.
Denote also by
k_{1},k_{2}
the elliptic modulus associated respectively with
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
.
Then, for all
\gamma\in{\mathbb{C}}
,

\sc(\gamma^{k}|k)=(1+k_{1})k_{2}^{\prime}\sc(\gamma^{k_{2}}|k_{2})\dn(\gamma^{k_{2}}|k_{2}).

The proof of this lemma consists in performing an ascending Landen transformation (see Section 3.9 of
[
56
]
) to go from
\tau
to
\tau_{1}=2\tau
and then the modular transformation
\tau\to\tau+1
to go from
\tau_{1}
to
\tau_{2}
.
The details are given below.
This implies the following.

Lemma 75
.

For
\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{C}}
,

\frac{\theta_{1,1}(\alpha,\tau)\theta_{1,0}(\beta,\tau)}{\theta_{1,0}(\alpha,\tau)\theta_{1,1}(\beta,\tau)}=\frac{\theta_{1,1}(\alpha,2\tau-1)\theta_{0,0}(\alpha,2\tau-1)\theta_{1,0}(\beta,2\tau-1)\theta_{0,1}(\beta,2\tau-1)}{\theta_{1,0}(\alpha,2\tau-1)\theta_{0,1}(\alpha,2\tau-1)\theta_{1,1}(\beta,2\tau-1)\theta_{0,0}(\beta,2\tau-1)}

This is a one-line corollary of Lemma
74
, because by definition of the
\sc
and
\dn
functions:

\frac{\theta_{1,1}(\alpha|\tau)\theta_{1,0}(\beta|\tau)}{\theta_{1,0}(\alpha|\tau)\theta_{1,1}(\beta|\tau)}=\frac{\sc(\alpha^{k}|\tau)}{\sc(\beta^{k}|\tau)}=\frac{\sc(\alpha^{k_{2}}|\tau_{2})\dn(\alpha^{k_{2}}|\tau_{2})}{\sc(\beta^{k_{2}}|\tau_{2})\dn(\beta^{k_{2}}|\tau_{2})}=\frac{\theta_{1,1}(\alpha|\tau_{2})\theta_{0,0}(\alpha|\tau_{2})\theta_{1,0}(\beta|\tau_{2})\theta_{0,1}(\beta|\tau_{2})}{\theta_{1,0}(\alpha|\tau_{2})\theta_{0,1}(\alpha|\tau_{2})\theta_{1,1}(\beta|\tau_{2})\theta_{0,0}(\beta|\tau_{2})}

Note that in principle we could obtain Lemma
75
directly with the same techniques since the ascending Landen transform also exists for theta functions, but we find the computations much easier with Jacobi elliptic functions.

Proof of Lemma
74
.

By the first point of Equation (22.2.2) of
[
25
]
, Equation (1.8.2) of
[
56
]
, the third point of Equation (22.2.2) of
[
25
]
and again the first point of Equation (22.2.2) of
[
25
]
:

2K(k)=\theta_{0,0}^{2}(0,\tau)=\theta_{0,0}^{2}(0,\tau_{1})+\theta_{1,0}^{2}(0,\tau_{1})=(1+k_{1})\theta_{0,0}^{2}(0,\tau_{1})=(1+k_{1})2K(k_{1})

so
Using Equations (3.9.28), (3.9.29) of
[
56
]
(the ascending Landen transform for
\sn
and
\cn
), we first obtain

\sc(\gamma^{k}|k)=(1+k_{1})\frac{\sn\big(\frac{\gamma^{k}}{1+k_{1}}\big|k_{1}\big)}{\cn\big(\frac{\gamma^{k}}{1+k_{1}}\big|k_{1}\big)\dn\big(\frac{\gamma^{k}}{1+k_{1}}\big|k_{1}\big)}=(1+k_{1})\frac{\sn(\gamma^{k_{1}}|k_{1}\big)}{\cn(\gamma^{k_{1}}|k_{1})\dn(\gamma^{k_{1}}|k_{1})}.

(82)

We now use the modular transformation
\tau\to\tau+1
.
First observe that by the first and second points of Equation (22.2.2) of
[
25
]
and Equation (
27
),

2K(k_{2})=\theta_{0,0}^{2}(0,\tau_{2})=\frac{\theta_{0,1}^{2}(0,\tau_{2})}{k_{2}^{\prime}}=\frac{\theta_{0,0}^{2}(0,\tau_{1})}{k_{2}^{\prime}}=\frac{2K(k_{1})}{k_{2}^{\prime}}.

Then, the modular transformation
\tau\to\tau+1
for elliptic functions (9.3.6)-(9.3.8) of
[
56
]
(which is a direct consequence of Equation (
27
) and the definition of the Jacobi elliptic functions) writes

\sn(\gamma^{k_{1}}|k_{1})=k_{2}^{\prime}\sd(\gamma^{k_{2}}|k_{2})\quad;\quad\cn(\gamma^{k_{1}}|k_{1})=k_{2}^{\prime}\cd(\gamma^{k_{2}}|k_{2})\quad;\quad\dn(\gamma^{k_{1}}|k_{1})=k_{2}^{\prime}\nd(\gamma^{k_{2}}|k_{2})

so Equation (
82
) gives

\sc(\gamma^{k}|k)=(1+k_{1})k_{2}^{\prime}\frac{\sd(\gamma^{k_{2}}|k_{2})}{\cd(\gamma^{k_{2}}|k_{2})\nd(\gamma^{k_{2}}|k_{2})}=(1+k_{1})k_{2}^{\prime}\sc(\gamma^{k_{2}}|k_{2})\dn(\gamma^{k_{2}}|k_{2}),

which concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎
